353 
Concordances 
The following three concordances correlate the three currently available arrangements of 
the fragments: 
(1) Numbers (1-854), not preceded by letters, refer to the numbering of the glass 
frames in the State Library in Berlin where the original fragments are preserved. The 
fragments were framed some time during the sixties, in any case after the erection of the 
Berlin Wall, since at that time Schlingloffhad the fragments (kept in bags? cf. introduction 
p. 4 7) in his possession. I was not able to obtain any information about the actual process 
of framing of the fragments; however, the numbers on the glass frames correspond to those 
on a handwritten list compiled by Waldschmidt's student, W. Clawiter. It is, therefore, 
possible that Clawiter, or perhaps even Waldschmidt himself, was involved in reframing 
the fragments. When several fragments are kept in a single frame, these are numbered in 
brackets from left to right and top to bottom; e.g., 9(1), 9(2) and 9(3) refer to the left, 
middle and right fragments in side "a" of the 9th frame respectively. 
(2) Numbers preceded by the letter "S." refer to the pages of Schlingloffs 
"Schnellhefter" on which black and white photos of the fragments have been glued. There 
are four such "Schnellhefter" and their number precedes the number of the respective 
page. When several photos are found on a single page, they are similarly numbered from 
left to right and from top to bottom. Thus, S. 1.1 0( 1) would refer to the left (or top) photo 
on the 1oth page of the first "Hefter". In an orange folder I have found ten loose pages with 
photos. These are referred to by "lp" (i.e., S.lp 1, etc.). A considerable number of the 
fragments are transcribed on subsequent or preceding pages of these photos; these are 
referred to by "tr." (=transcription). As mentioned above, the photos reflect the state of 
the fragments after World War II, but before their framing. Therefore, the comparison 
between the original fragments and the photos is important for at least four reasons. First, 
in a few cases a fragment was broken before it was framed and the photo represents it still 
unbroken ( cf. e.g., frg. 1 0). Further, in very few cases the script has deteriorated only 
recently and the photo preserves a clearer picture of the ak.Jaras. Thirdly, the comparison 
allows one to check whether some fragments were lost only recently (i.e., between the end 
of the fifties or the early sixties and the present). A one-to-one correspondence between 
the photos and the fragments proved impossible to establish. When I could not identify a 
fragment that corresponded to a given photo, the latter is marked by "u" (=unidentified). 
Most of the unidentified photos are very small and sometimes also illegible. They may 
well have corresponding fragments, but are of no consequence, and I would have had to 
spend an inordinate amount of time to identify them. Certain larger photos, however, can 
be said with certainty to have no correspondence among the surviving fragments (for as 
soon as a single word was legible I could identify the photo with the help of the word 
index, etc.). These are the photos on 2.106, 2.110, 2.114 and most of the photos on 4.135-
153, 4.157, 4.186-189 and 4.194-195. Significantly, all of these "lost fragments" are 

=== PAGE 5 ===
354 
written in a different script and do not belong to our manuscript. Some of them bear close 
resemblance to some of the fragments presented in the appendix "Fragments by Different 
Hands." It is possible, therefore, that the fragments corresponding to these photos were 
removed from SHT 810 on purpose. An attempt to scan the photos and present them here 
proved impractical because of their inferior quality. Fourthly, the arrangement of the 
photos represents, at least to some extent, Schlinglofr s early attempt to arrange the 
fragments meaningfully. This may prove useful for those who would like to continue the 
work on the Spitzer manuscript, especially as I did not have sufficient time to explore this 
avenue of research. 
(3) Some of the pages in Schlinglofrs "Hefter" refer to an earlier classification with a 
distinction of A and B. Schlingloffhimselfhas no recollection of this classification and 
Spitzer does not refer to it. It is certainly the earliest classification and in fact may go back 
to Else Liiders' arrangement of the fragments. This classification, not examined by me, 
may also prove useful for subsequent research. 
Concordance I: Frames=t>S. 9> AlB

=== PAGE 32 ===
381 
Word Index 
References are given by fragment number (=frame number), side (a or b) and line. When more 
than one fragment appears in a frame, the fragment is further specified between I /, e.g., 
165a/20/1 refers to the first line of the 20th fragment (counting from left to right and top to 
bottom) on side a of frame 165. However, in some cases when only two small fragments appear 
in a frame, the reference to a fragment number in"//" was considered superfluous and not given. 
Numbers preceded by "n" refers to the page number, etc., of the Spitzer Nachlass, those 
preceded by "b" to fragments from the Berezovsky collection and those preceded by "dh" to 
fragments written by a different hand. 
Uncertain readings are indicated by *. 
Compounds appear under each of their members; when a given word is the first member 
of a compound, the compound appears as an independent lemma; otherwise it appears as a sub-
heading of that word; e.g. tadartha appears as a sub-heading of artha, arthahfna as an 
independent lemma after artha. The original spelling is kept whenever possible, but cross-
references are given at the appropriate place; e.g. tirttha appears under tirttha, but a cross-
reference is given under artha. Verbal forms appear under the root, as do verbal forms with 
upasargas (e.g. upalabhyate appears under lambh and sub-heading upa). Pronouns are grouped 
together except when in adverbial form (e.g. kasmif[lscit appears under k, but kvacit as an 
independent lemma, tasmtit under t, but tatas as an independent lemma). If the same reference 
is repeated, this means that the word appears twice in the same line. 
This index was prepared with the help of the program "Bhela" written by the late 
Professor Ronald Emmerick who was kind enough to make numerous changes in his program 
to suit my needs. It was only after generating the index that I realized that the database became 
corrupt at some point. By that time Professor Emmerick, who was probably the only one capable 
of repairing the database, had passed away. My friend Burkhard Quessel spared neither time nor 
effort in an attempt to save the database, but to no avail. Fortunately, I had kept an older version 
of the index, which also turned out to be based on a damaged database, but less severely 
damaged than the final version. I used this version as a basis for the index and spent a 
considerable amount of time fixing it, but a few mistakes may remain, for which I apologize to 
the user.

435 
Towards a Reconstruction of the Spitzer Manuscript 
The purpose of this essay is to present a partial reconstruction and interpretation of the last 
portion of the manuscript, from fol. 369 until the manuscript's presumed end ca. fol. 414. 
Large portions of this reconstruction have been published before and are reproduced here 
with only slight modifications. 1 
I will begin with the first three pages of Spitzer's Nachlass. Two of the fragments 
were also transcribed on p. 28,2 of the Nachlass, two on p. 35/ and one on p. 644; the 
variants have been pointed out in the notes. Further, whenever available I note the variants 
in Watanabe's transcriptions as published by Miyasaka.5 Note that I did not change 
Spitzer's transcriptions and that sometimes better readings appear in the notes rather than 
in the main text. In the discussion below I also suggest some conjectures in pointed 
brackets and add punctuation to facilitate the reading. 
[Page 1] 
Blatt 369 
a. 
[1] khalv api6 kin vijani[.]. . . .(dul:t)kha[l11] 
tathaeparisi~tan(i) dul:tkha111 ca 
duJ:lkh[a]tatpraptam8 parisi~tany api duJ:lkhan tasmad9 duJ:lkhadarsanat parisi~!adarsana111
10 
duJ:lkhadars ...... . 
1 Cf. my "Lost Fragments from the Spitzer Manuscript" and "Towards a reconstruction of the Spitzer 
Manuscript- The dialectical portion." 
2 P. 28 contains the transcription of another small fragment which contains the word parisi~{ani and thus 
seems to belong to folio 369, but Spitzer did not include it here. 
3 Next to the transcription of these two fragments Spitzer refers to an unspecified location: "6 unten". 
4 I have numbered the pages in the order in which they were placed in the folder. 
5 Cf. Miyasaka, "Kyoryobu no danpen." 
6 One of the most conspicuous phraseological characteristics of the Spitzer Manuscript is the frequent use 
of khalv api, cf. the introduction to vol. I, p. 33. 
7 Miyasaka, p. 676, 27bl, corrects: tatha (ta)tparisi~{ani. Note that Miyasaka's transcription of this line 
begins with ...... kham, i.e. without khalv api kin vijanf[.]. 
8 Miyasaka's transcription, p. 676, differs considerably; it omits duJ:tkhalJl ca and reads dukhat (sic.) 
praptam. Read: ... duJ:tkham. tat praptam. 
9 Miyasaka, p. 676, reads tasman na. 
10 Miyasaka' s transcription of this line ends here.

The transcriptions reproduced above cover fragments belonging to six consecutive folios 
(369-374). Although not all fragments bear folio numbers,91 or complete folio numbers,92 
Spitzer must have considered the fragments to be parts of consecutive folios, and his 
assumption can now be confirmed with greater certainty. 93 The discussion preserved in the 
fragments ranges over three chapters: The first, titled anupurvabhisamayaparfk~a, ends 
in 371b2; the second, titled (prakrti)prabhasvarapratijfiopalambhika continues up to 
374bl; of the third only two and a half lines remain (374bl-3), but some more fragments 
which deal with the same subject and which must have belonged to folio 375 could be 
identified and are reproduced below. The topics of all three chapters constitute points of 
debate among various schools of Conservative Buddhism. 
1) The first chapter concerns the question whether the four noble truths are fully com-
prehended successively or at once. The debate centers on the so-called abhisamayavada.94 
In his classification of the points of controversy, Bareau has pointed out that according to 
the Andhakas, the late Mahasanghikas, the Sarvastivadins, the Sammatiyas and the 
Bhadrayaniyas the full comprehension of the four noble truths is gradual, whereas 
according to the Theravadins, the Vibhajyavadins, the Mahisasakas and the Maha-
sanghikas it occurs at once, that is, in a single cognitive act.95

90 On p. 64: 1 //. 
91 For instance, the 3rd and the 41h folio that I consider to be folios 371 and 372. 
92 For instance, the second folio reads "37?", but its placement after fol. 369 makes clear that Spitzer 
considered it to be fol. 370. 
93 We can confirm that "Blatt?"= 374 by going backwards from folio 376 which is preserved as fragment 
137; parts of folio 375 are reproduced below. The rest follows easily. 
94 The term abhisamayaviida was coined by Frauwallner in his remarkable study of this doctrine in 
Dharmasri's *Abhidharmahrdaya or *Abhidharmasiira; c£ E. Frauwallner, "Abhidharma-Studien. III. Der 
Abhisamayavada1)," esp. p. 102. With all respect due to Frauwallner, the name he chose has at least one 
considerable disadvantage, namely, it is too general and can be applied to Dharmasri's theory as well as to 
its opposite (ekiibhisamaya(viida) or yugapadabhisamaya(viida)). In what follows I retain the longer, but 
ernie terms anupiirviibhisamaya(viidalin) and ekiibhisamaya(viida/in).

respectively. The discussion here presupposes the *Abhdharmahrdaya or *Abhidharmasiira ofDharmasri 
or Dharmasre~!hin; cf. Introduction, n. 13 7. The same topic has been recently investigated in relation to SHT 
649; cf. N. Aramaki, "A Fragment ofthe Caturiigamopadesa ofParsva?".

Folio 369 begins somewhere in the middle of the third section of the chapter 
anupiirviibhisamayaparfk~ii. The Piirvapak~in (369al) seems to argue that the last three 
noble truths are similar to the first noble truth of suffering.96 And (the apprehension or the 
object of the noble truth of) suffering is suffering. Thus, the following obtains: The rest, 
i.e., the other three noble truths, are also suffering. Therefore, by seeing (the noble truth 
of) suffering one sees the rest. (I read: <yathii> ( duJ:t )kha[ 1J'l] tathii t<at> parisi~{iin( i ). 
duJ:tkha1Jt ca duJ:tkh[ a]<1Jt>. tat priiptam: pari-si~{iiny api duJ:tkhan. tasmiid duJ:tkha-
darsaniit parisi~{adarsana1Jt).
97 
The next line (369a2) appears to contain the reply of the Siddhantin, who derives 
the opposite conclusion from the alleged similarity among the truths: (If it is said that) the 
second, third and fourth truths are like the first truth, (then) the following obtains: the first 
truth is like the other three. But (the other three truths are) not suffering. (Thus, the first 
truth would also not be suffering) (yathii khalv api duJ:tkham tathii t<at>parisi~fiini. tat 
priiptam: yathii parisi~{iini tathii duJ:tkham, na ca duJ:tkha ... ). The reason why the 
Siddhantin rejects the identity between the first and the other truths is not clear here. I 
assume that it is because the first truth is itself suffering whereas the others, or at least the 
third and fourth truths, are not considered to be suffering, but this is not stated explicitly 
in the fragment and I was also unable to identify the exact Sarvastivada position on this 
point. According to the Sarvastivadin the third truth is asa1Jtskrta, but the other three are 
not.98 If one considers that everything which is produced ( sa1Jtskrta) is duJ:tkha, one could 
say that the third truth is not suffering, but the other three are. If this is the Sarvastivada 
position, then our text would seem to be incompatible with it. 99 
The discussion continues on the verso side of the leaf (369b ). In the first line there 
may be a statement to the effect that the apprehension of the four truths does not occur at 
once, "on one occasion" ( ekasamaye ). In the second line it is argued that it can be 
admitted100 that one fully understands suffering as suffering, but that this does not imply 
96 The reason for the statement is missing. Note also that here the discrepancy between the transcriptions of 
Spitzer and of Watanabe/Miyasaka is considerable. 
97 I follow here Spitzer's transcription. Note that Watanabe/Miyasaka transcrib~ tasman na instead of 
tasmad. If the W atanabe/Miyasaka transcription is correct, then we obviously deal with a siddhanta 
statement. Indeed, in the discussion that follows, the words tasman na dul;lkhadarsanat parisi~!adarsanam 
occur repeatedly, and this in itself would make the Watanabe/Miyasaka transcription more probable. 
However, the immediately preceding statement that "the rest are also suffering" (pariSi~tany api duiJkham) 
is clearly the purvapalcya in the discussion. 
98 Cf. Bareau, op. cit., p. 138 (th. 8); cf. alsop. 197 (th. 32) for the Sariputriibhidharma, p. 100 (th. 10) for 
the Purvasaila, and p. 221 (th. 56) for the Theravada. 
99 According to Bareau, op. cit., the Hetuvadins (p. 246, th. 3) considered all asal'{tSkrtas except miirga to 
be suffering, whereas the Theravadins (p. 233, th. 166) seem to argue that all noble truths are not suffering. 
The position of the Sarvastivada is unknown to me. 
100 I interpret yadi khalv api as yadi nama ("let it be admitted") and the enclitic ca of the next sentence in 
an adversative sense. Otherwise the next phrase would be part of a conditional clause introduced withyadi 
khalv api and be connected with ca in its conjunctive sense. It is also possible that the apodosis (i.e., the 

=== PAGE 95 ===
444 
that by seeing suffering one sees the other truths too ( yadi khalv api ( du )(lkhaiJl du(lkhat[ o] 
( 'bhi)[samiigacchat](i). na ca bhavati du(lkhadarsaniit pariSi~!a ... ).In the third line the 
Siddhantin continues to argue that by seeing suffering one does not see the other truths 
because the false view of the skandhas as the Self ( satkiiyadr~!i) does not have as its object 
the suppression of suffering and the way that leads to the suppression of suffering ( <na> 
bhavati du(lkhadarsaniit parisi~fadarsanaiJl. na hi nirodhamiirgiilambanii satkiiyadr~!il:z). 
In other words, seeing the third and fourth truth does not involve the false view about the 
Self. This clearly implies that seeing suffering (i.e., the first truth) does involve the false 
view about the Self. Thus, these two (which do and do not involve the false view about the 
Self) cannot occur at the same time. Therefore, the full comprehension of the four noble 
truths occurs gradually (tasmiid anupurviibhisamaya<(l> ). If this is the correct 
interpretation of the fragment, then the Spitzer manuscript expresses a point of view that 
seems to be incompatible with the established doctrine of the Sarvastivada. For it is stated 
in the Abhidharmakosa that the satkiiyadr~!i is abandoned or eliminated by seeing the first 
truth. 101 I do not know how to resolve this contradiction. Perhaps the author of the Spitzer 
manuscript means that the elimination of the satkiiyadr~!i by seeing the first truth involves 
its apprehension as something false; however, it no longer finds support in the seeing of 
the third and fourth truth. Thus, it cannot appear again, not even as something that has to 
be discarded. Perhaps this suggestion slightly stretches the common usage of the word 
iilambana (objective support of the cognition), but the only other alternative I could think 
of, namely, that our manuscript provides an unknown variant to the anupurviibhisamaya-
viida seems even less probable. Of course, as long as the text is fragmentary, there is no 
certainty as to how the argument is to be interpreted.

=== PAGE 99 ===
448 
notably, the Mahasailghikas, the Vibhajyavadins and in the Sliriputrlibhidharma, 118 that 
consciousness is luminous by nature ( citta1Jl prakrtiprabhlisvaram), but can be defiled 
(kli~!a, upakli~!a) by the defilements (klesa, upaklesa) or liberated (vipramukta) from 
them. In this sense it is said that "consciousness accompanied by desire is liberated" 
( sarliga1Jl citta1]l vimuccati). 119 
This doctrine is based on a passage in Anguttara Nikliya I.6120: pabhassaram ida1Jl 
bhikkhave citta1]l ta! ca kho ligantukehi upakkilesehi upakkili!!han. "This consciousness, 
monks, is luminous, and it is defiled by adventitious defilements." 
In spite of this and similar passages, the major schools of Conservative Buddhism, 
especially the Theravada and the Sarvastivada, reject this doctrine. 121 Consciousness is not 
luminous or pure by nature; it is defiled by passions and actions, and the connection with 
them has to be severed. If consciousness were luminous by nature, it could not become 
defiled by the defilements. Further, the dharmas perish at every moment. Therefore, it is 
not the defiled luminous consciousness which is liberated. Rather, when the connection 
with the defilements is severed, afuture122 consciousness is liberated, i.e., the cognition 
in the next moment, which is a cognition of the Arhat (asaik~ya-citta) arises free from 
defilements. 
In accordance with this opinion the Siddhantin (371b3) states that the upaklesas123 
are not adventitious to consciousness as long as both (consciousness and defilements) exist 
at present( ... upaklesaJ:t. tadubhayaprat(yu)t(pan)n( a)tve ligantukatva1]l na bhavati ... ).

is not perceived ( ... 124 prabhasvaratvam upalabdham). If the Piirvapak~in replies that the 
luminous consciousness is defiled (yad ucyate prabhasvaram upaklisyata iti yadi ... ) (and 
this is the reason why its natural luminosity is not perceived in its present state; cf. 
pratyutpanna ), the Siddhantin answers that what is luminous cannot be defiled ( ...

373al refers again to the opponent, for whom being consciousness and being

The proponent must have rejected the construed difference between consciousness and 
luminosity. Further, he could have argued that under the assumption of such a difference, 
a defilement, if it comes into contact with a luminous consciousness, would become 
luminous itself, clearly an inadmissible consequence, just as that the luminous 
consciousness too, inasmuch as it comes into contact with a defilement, is supposedly 
defiled ( (pra )bhasvaram apy upaklesam anuprliptam kli ... ). This interpretation is based on 
a parallel passage in the Vibhii~li, as summarized by Lamotte. 129 
In 373a3 the opponent possibly attempts to establish the difference between 
luminosity and consciousness by taking recourse to the factor of time. Consciousness 
could have been luminous in the past ((pra)bhasvaram asft130) and become defiled later 
on. The opponent may have understood this difference as a difference in state ( cf. avastha 
in 373bl). I assume that the proponent now repeats his previous argument in his reply, 
namely, that if a state of consciousness is not something different from consciousness 
itself, then when the state is that of being defiled, consciousness is defiled. If, on the other 
hand, the state would be different from consciousness itself, defiling is due to an 
interaction between two different entities ( cf .... anyenanyasya ... ). Thus, the relationship 
between consciousness and the defiled state is the same as that between consciousness and 
the defilement, for the defilement is also different from consciousness ( upaklesasya py 
any ... ), and thus consciousness itself would not be affected by the defiled state. The 
argument must have been more complicated and must have also taken into account 
whether a state lasts for some time( ... (a)vasthii131 bhavati. athavati~fhate ... ). 
The opponent may have argued that the proponent does not understand what it 
means to be defiled. Because consciousness is defiled by the defilements, it is suffering, 
and being defiled by something else it becomes itself a defilement ( ( upakli )syate 
tasmii<d> d[u](~kh) ... syanyenopakli~fasyopaklesatvaf{l ... ).The defilement occurs by

As for a possible arrangement of these fragments I suggest the following. Since number 
4 is likely to come after 3, and the figure 4 (in 59b) appears in line 1 while the two figures 
for 3 (in SOb and 130a) appear in line 2, 59b must belong to the verso, and SOb and 130a 
to the recto of folio 375. I suggest also that 130 be placed to the right of 59 because 3 in 
130a concludes the siddhlinta reply to the parvapalcya numbered 3 and concluded in SOb, 
and thus enough space should separate the two fragments to allow for the siddhlinta 
statement or argument. S54 seems closely related to 59 and I tentatively place it to its left 
(yathli buddha (evam lirryaJ:z?) ... yathlirrya/:l evam buddhal:z). There is no way to deter-
mine where 51, 242 and 54S may belong and therefore I do not include them. The proba-
ble arrangement of SO, 59, 130 and S54 can be represented as follows. Note, however, 
that the spaces between the fragments are misleading; the original leaf may have been 
some 40 em wide.

Concerning the content of these fragments, the discussion begins on the fragment pre-
served only in Spitzer's transcription with the statement of the opponent's position, 
namely, that the disciples are endowed with both the general and specific qualities of the 
teacher, and thus the general and specific qualities of the Buddha are present in his 
disciples, the sriivakas' and therefore "we" see that he too is in the san..gha (ye iiciiryya-
gul}asiimanyavise~ayuktiil;l sarJ7..ghe . . . ~ayuktal;l. atal;l pasyiimal;l bhagaviin a pi sa1Jtghe 
iti). The Siddhantin's rejection of this position, which was divided into at least five 
sections, cannot be reconstructed in detail. In folio 375 only one argument seems rela-
tively clear. The Siddhantin denies that an enlightened disciple (Arhat, which I assume is 
referred to with the word iirya) is similar to the Buddha (yathiiryyaJ:z eva1J1 buddhal;l .. .) . 134 
His reason, or one of his reasons, for the rejection of the similarity is that if this were the 
case the Arhat too would be a Buddha ((ii}ry[y]a e[v]alfl [buddha ii}panna .. ). 
In folio 376 the Siddhantin must have argued that the mere fact that the Buddha 
and the Arhats share some general as well as specific qualities or properties does not 
prove that the Buddha can be seen in the saligha. Everything has similarities and dissimi-
larities with everything (sarvvasya c<a> sarvve!la siidharmmyavaidha(r)myam ast(i)). 
The opponent may have replied that the adduced similarities are not mere accidental 
properties, and that there is an essential similarity between the Buddha and the Arhats 
which consists in their connection with the same general and specific qualities of moral 
conduct (cf. 376v2: sacciiryagul}[a]-siim<li>nyavise~a-yogal;l
135
). The Siddhantin's reply 
is largely lost, but one can see that he used two types of argument in this connection. The 
one (376vl) charges the opponent with committing the fallacy of pratijiiiiviruddha. This 
word is certainly used here as a technical term, but the context does not allow us to 
determine which of the various meanings that appear in the early manuals of debate136 is 
intended here. Moreover, it has to be noted that viruddha can be used in the sense of 
"contradictory," that is, pratijiiliviruddha does not necessarily mean "contradicted by the 
thesis"; it can also mean "contradictory to the thesis." My guess is that the term is em-
ployed here in this sense, but even so it is impossible to determine exactly what is contra-
dictory to the thesis. 137 
The other argument refers to the opponent's proof(or inference? *siidhana) and 
points out the absence ofthe·property to be proved and the proving property (s(ii)dhya-

siidha[kii]bhiiva). Unfortunately the proof itself does not appear in the fragment, or more 
precisely, only part of it appears in the initial statement (ye iiciiryagul)a-, etc.). 
The same topic is probably referred to by Bareau, namely, "[Le] Buddha est inclus 
dans la Communaute". 138 Among the schools which endorse the thesis that the Buddha is 
included in the community, Bareau mentions the Sarvastivada without, however, specifi-
cation of a thesis number, and I could not find this thesis in his presentation of the 
Sarvastivada. Further, according to the materials presented by Bareau the question 
whether the Buddha is included in the saligha seems to have arisen in the context of 
donation (i.e., whether a donation made to the saligha is also eo ipso a donation to the 
Buddha), and in the present fragments there is no trace of such a discussion, although, of 
course, not enough fragments remain to justify an argument ex silentio with any certainty. 
IfBareau's attribution of this thesis to the Sarvastivada is correct, this would be a 
reason to reject the identification of the present discussion as a treatment of this topic be-
cause what should accordingly be endorsed by the Sarvastivada appears here as a piirva-
pak~a. A possible solution to this problem would be to distinguish between the thesis "the 
Buddha is in (i.e., part of) the saligha" and the thesis "the Buddha is perceived in (i.e., 
through?) the saligha" discussed here. Alternatively, one may assume that the Spitzer 
Manuscript does not represent a text of the Sarvastivada school. However, I would 
hesitate to take such a step without further substantiation because practically all the 
Sanskrit manuscripts in the Turfan collection are assumed to belong to the Sarvastivada 
school, and as I have tried to show in the introduction the author of the manuscript seems 
to admit the existence of past and future objects. 139 
I could not identify any fragments from fols. 377-378. The assignment offrgs. 98 
and 13 8(7 /7) to fol. 3 79 may be justified as follows. These two fragments preserve the 
end of the chapter bodhibauddhavyavaise~ika-prakaral)a and the begining of the chapter 
on cittaviprayukta-jfiiina. 140 The latter has clearly started before fol. 3 80 and thus the two 
fragments could belong to fol. 377,378 or 379. The assumption that the fragments belong 
to fol. 3 77 can be discarded because such an assignment would leave very little space (at 
most half a folio) for the entire chapter bodhibauddhavyavaise#ka-prakara~Ja. The 
assumption that the two fragments belong to fol. 378 is perhaps not impossible, but if the 
length of text sections 5 and 6 in fol. 380vl-2 is anything to go by (barely more than a 
line for both sections), the chapter on cittaviprayukta-jfiiina could not have started before 
fol. 3 79. Thus we may present the following sequence of fragments:

It is difficult to make sense of what is left from the remnants of the chapter 
bodhibauddhavyavaise#ka-prakararza. As its name indicates, the chapter is concerned 
with the distinction between cognitions and what is to be known, i.e., their objects or 
referents. However, the discussion is clearly not similar to, say, the one on the twelve 
liyatanas or eighteen dhatus. It seems rather that the distinction the author is concerned 
with here is an epistemological one: The types of cognition discussed seem to be true and 
false cognitions (mithyiibuddhi, samyagbuddhi), doubt and determination (saiJlsaya, 
vyavasiiya), etc. The objects of these cognitions are determined as existing or non-existing. 
Pol. *379rl: in respect to each single (type of object a various types of cognition may 
arise:) doubt, determination, etc.(ek[ai]kasotpatteJ:l saiJlsayavyavasiiyiin[it]y. Ill).

existing; (a certain cognition)

Unfortunately, the beginning of the discussion, that is, most of the first two lines (fol. 
*383r3-v1 ), is lost, and thus the initial formulation of the positions of the opponent and the 
proponent are missing. Two terms that recur throughout the discussion are already present

the antithesis consisted in. A third term that also occurs very frequently is vipak.Ja, which 
I consider to refer here to a counter-example, 149 although a concrete counter-example has 
not been preserved in the fragments. There is only a single fragment among those that 
belong to the present context, though not the immediate context, from which a concrete 
thesis could perhaps be derived, namely, frg. 110 (= fol. *389(?); cf. p. 478): lines 110r3 
and 110v1 mention sarvvamastitviibhiiva. 150 Because this seems to be the opponent's 
position, one could assume that the proponent's thesis is that everything exists ( sarvam 
asti). However, although such an interpretation cannot not be ruled out completely, I think 
that it is improbable. Although a relatively large number of fragments of this discussion 
remains, the topics of the thesis, antithesis and counter-example are never even alluded to, 
and one would expect their formulation in this case. I suggest, therefore, that the content 
of the thesis and counter-thesis was indeed not formulated. In other words, what the 
opponent in fol. *383v ff. claims is that a thesis is automatically invalidated by the very 
existence of its antithesis. If this suggestion is accepted, then it may be reasonably 
assumed that the discussion in the Spitzer fragments runs along similar lines as those 
preserved in chapter 1 of the Tarkasiistra. 
Before continuing with the discussion itself, I would like to point out a very 
peculiar stylistic characteristic that is conspicuous both in the Spitzer fragments and in 
chapter 1 of the TS, namely, the use of indirect speech. Of course, indirect speech is not 
unheard of in Classical Sanskrit-Speijer even gives a remarkable example from Kalidasa 
where iti is used to conclude indirect speech151-, but it is quite rare, and not only in 
philosophical texts. It is to Tucci's credit that he did not shrink from keeping the indirect 
mode in his re-translation of the Tarkasiistra into Sanskrit: 
TS 3.10-11: mama vacanam anyiiyyam152 iti ced bhavatocyate, tato bhaviin ajfia iti 
spa.J{am.

occurs) even though this theory is already known to the author of the Tarkasiistra. For the meaning of 
counter-example cf. pw s. v.

The first chapter of the TS has been hitherto almost completely ignored. I am not aware 
of any publication touching upon this chapter since Tucci published his translation in 
1929,155 and Tucci himself did not pay much attention to it. 156 The reason for this neglect 
may be that the chapter upholds a position that is seemingly quite absurd. The text begins 
abruptly as follows (3.3-5): "If you think that our statement is inappropriate, your 
statement too is inappropriate. If your statement is inappropriate, then our statement is 
appropriate."157 Just as in the Spitzer fragments, a concrete statement, whether appropriate 
or inappropriate, is never specified. Indeed, this does not seem to matter. Rather, the author 
may be concerned here with the relationship between statements as such: Any two 
statements that stand in contradiction or opposition to each other would do. The author of 
the TS argues that his statement is appropriate just because it is a statement. E.g. 4.11-12: 
"Furthermore, if our statement [were to be] inappropriate, it would not be a statement at 
all. If it is a statement, it is not at all inappropriate."158 
Similarly, he argues that his statement is appropriate just because it exists: "If there is a 
statement I if the statement exists, then it is known [to be] appropriate."159 
Even inappropriate statements are said to be intrinsically (svatafl) appropriate:

"Furthermore, what is inappropriate is appropriate by itself (svataJ:t). Therefore, what is 
inappropriate does not exist."160 
In the same manner the author argues: "What is inappropriate does not appear. Is a 
statement different or not different from the absence of appropriateness? If it is not 
different, the statement too is absent. Thus, how could you say that my statement is 
inappropriate? If it is different, then the statement is appropriate. 161 Thus, how could you 
say that my statement is inappropriate?"162 
One could elaborate further, but the above suffices to make the argument clear. 
Whoever the author of chapter I of the TS may have been, his stand seems to appear as that 
of an opponent in the Spitzer fragments. Of course, I do not intend to imply a direct 
connection and claim that the author of the fragments knew the TS. However, the opponent 
in the Spitzer fragments clearly argues along lines similar to those of the proponent in the 
TS. 
The discussion in the fragments is quite lengthy and contained at least twenty-two 
steps that are presented as corresponding text-sections. In fact, probably thirty-seven or 
more steps are enumerated in the manuscript. It is to be noted, however, that this 
enumeration is continuous for piirvapak~a and siddhtinta; up to step six piirvapak~a 
statements seem to be numbered, from step seven onwards siddhtinta statements.

Even though the numbers on the leaf are hardly legible and the discussion on identity and 
difference could have been taken up in a different context as well, the probability that this 
fragment belongs to the present context remains quite high. Already in fol. *385r the issue 
of identity and difference has been raised; the same issue is also apparent in fol. 386v and 
in other fragments that in my opinion belong to this chapter, notably in frg. 110 (part of 
fol. *389(?), cf. below, p. 478). 
Unfortunately, too little remains of folios 386-*388 to allow for reconstruction of 
the discussion. One can clearly see, though, that the issue of identity and difference is 
discussed in relation to that of similarity and dissimilarity, but the details elude me. 
However, I believe that it is not incidental that chapter 1 of the TS also contains a 
discussion on the identity and difference of the position and the counter-position. This 

=== PAGE 128 ===
477 
strengthens my assumption that closely related ideas are presented in both texts. Thus, as 
already mentioned, chapter 1 of the TS probably provides a glimpse of the discussion in 
the Spitzer fragments. In the following, I translate only the beginning of the discussion in 
the TS on the basis of Tucci's translation ( 5 .18f.): 
What has been said by you, namely, that my statement is different (from yours[?]) because 
it is at variance, 
172 that [will] now be settled/determined after investigating [it] together 
with you. If someone would voice a different [statement], then a fault would be his. 
[Conversely] your thesis differs from our thesis. If this [statement] of yours has been 
voiced, then [a statement] different [from mine] has been voiced. Therefore, it is you who 
commits a fault. If your object (i.e., the referent of your statement) is different from what 
is said by us, then the fault of being different is yours, and not mine. If [your statement] 
is not different, then it is exactly identical with my position. Thus, there is no difference. 
If it is said that my [statement] is different, this is false. And something different is not 
different from something different. 173 Thus, there is no difference ( ananyatva). If 
something different is different from something different, it would not be something 
different, just as a man who is different from a cow is not a cow. If something is different 
from [the point of view of] a different thing, then [because the different thing would not 
be different, it] would be one [with the other thing]. If [it is] one [with it], [it is] not 
different [from it]. Thus, why is it said [by you that my statement] is different [from 
yours]? And therefore, this [statement of mine] is appropriate. Thus, relying on an 
appropriate [statement] I debate with you. Therefore, I say [it] differently. If there would 
be no difference between the two of us, then there [would be] no debate with you, but I 
[would] state exactly your object (i.e., the same referent as that of your statement). 174 
Before moving on to the next group of fragments, which must belong to the following

In frg. 448+99 (= fol. *390(?)r2), text-section 5, the Siddhantin concedes that his 
statement may be false, but probably denies the opponent's thesis nevertheless. Although 
the arguments are not preserved, the Siddhantin may well have replied that just as the 
existence of a position does not imply that it is true, the falsity of a position does not imply 
that it does not exist. This concession, however, is only provisional (yadi khalv api = yady 
api) and on the verso the Siddhantin concludes text-section 5 with the statement that what 
he has said is appropriate ([ta]smad upapanna1Jl yad mayoktaf[l). In the line previous to 
this statement the topic of identity and difference is taken up again. It has to be noted that 
a number of other fragments also preserve discussions involving the pair anya and ananya, 
some definitely belonging to other parts of the manuscript, others probably to the present 
context. One of them is frg. 676 which may even have been part of the present leaf and 
could be tentatively placed to the left of frg. 448+99. It also contains the number 5, with 
the result of two "5"s and the same problem as posed by the two "2"s on fol. *389(?). The 
details, however, remain unclear to me and I cannot determine if the enumeration is 
perhaps duplicated according to purvapak~a and siddhiinta.

the so-called vtirttika-style, in which the argument is first expressed or captured by means

of doubt continued to the first line of the subsequent leaf where the stock example of doubt

discussed and we find the remnants of the well-known question how, if everythipg is 
established by pramtif}as, the pramli!J-as themselves are established. If they are established 
by other pramiif}as, an infinite regress ( anavasthii) would result. In line 3 one can observe 
the alternative explanation, namely, that the pramtif}as establish each other, e.g., tradition 
is established by analogy, analogy (by inference, inference by perception, and perception 
by tradition). 187 For these arguments the parallels in the Vigrahavytivartanf (e.g., VV 32, 
51 188) are well-known. On side a, which I consider to be the verso, one can discern the 
opinion that the pramiif}as are capable of establishing themselves as well as their objects, 
just as a lamp is capable of illuminating itself as well as its object (i.e., one does not need 
an additional lamp to see a lamp). This argument too is well-known from VV 33, NS

It is customary for the topic of prama~Jas to be dealt with in the context of debate, but we 
cannot automatically assign all such fragments to the present context because the topic of 
prama~Jas was discussed at least twice in the text: once in fols. ca. 133-134190 and once 
here in the vicinity of fol. *395 (i.e., before fol. 398 but after fol. 393). (This could be a 
further indication that the manuscript contained two or more works and was in fact a 
compilation.) The larger fragments seem to belong to the former passage. If one relies on 
external criteria such as the height of the fragments and line spacing, only two fragments 
(frgs. 474+63 reproduced above) concerned with prama~Jas can be assigned with 
reasonable certainty to the present context. 
The text has certainly recognized (in both passages) the validity of four pramaiJaS, 
namely, perception, inference, verbal testimony and analogy or comparison. It is 
interesting to point out that in terminology it seems to follow the same tradition as that of 
the CS: the word aupamya appears in three fragments (frgs. 85b2, 132b(1)2, 474b3 and 
probably also in frg. 63b3) whereas the word upamana is never mentioned in the Spitzer 
fragments. 191 Similarly we find the word aitihya (frg. 63b3), but not sabda in a context 
where it clearly refers to sabdaprama~Ja. It is difficult to ascertain whether the order in 
which the pramaiJaS were presented and discussed was the same as the one in the CS, that 
is, with aitihya appearing before aupamya (cf. CS, Vi 8.27). A small fragment (frg. 136b5, 
cf. p. 4 78) seems to discuss aupamya on one side and sadhana, the next topic in the text, 
on the other. This could indicate the same order as that of the CS, and in this respect the 
text would differ both from the NS and from the UH which recognize the same four 
prama~Jas, but present and discuss analogy before verbal testimony .192 However, in frg. 
19° Cf. my "A Note on Nagarjuna and the Naiyayikas."

aupamya. 
192 Cf. NS 1.1.3ff.; cf. also UH, p. 13.3-4: atha katividhaf!t pramli7Jam. caturvidhaf!l pramli7Jam. pratyak~am 
anumlinam upamlinam ligamas ceti.

Of course, one is tempted to assume that the fragment refers to two of the characteristics 
of perception as enumerated in NS 1.1.4. However, a note of caution needs to be added 
here, not only because the reading is not absolutely certain, but also because the two words 
are not found next to each other and are not even on the same side of the leaf. Also, this 
is the only occurrence of these two terms. The compound vyavastiyiitmaka does not appear 
in the extant fragments; the word vyavasiiya occurs several times but nowhere in apparent 
relation to perception. The proximity of the words avyabhictirin and acakra 193 indicates 
that the text used the famous case of a circle of fire as an example for false perception or 
sensory illusion. Several fragments (notably frgs. 340 and 602) actually mention 
altitacakra, but I could not determine their place in the text. 
Further, there is some probability that the word indriytirthasannikar~a or a similar 
expression containing the word indriya was part of the definition of perception. First, there 
is a fragment (frg. 132(2))194 explaining how the senses are inferred. However, I am quite 
certain that this fragment belongs to fol. 133 and that the example was used to illustrate 
inference, i.e., that this fragment was not part of a discussion on perception. The same 
holds good for two relatively large fragments that deal with comparison and will not be 
presented here. There is only one fragment that I am quite certain belongs to the present 
context, namely, frg. 136(3/5) (side b must be recto). It is a very small fragment, but

A relatively large number of fragments concerning the topic of proofhave been preserved. 
Mostly they are quite small and hardly anything can be understood from them except that 
the subject matter concerns the question of whether sound is permanent or impermanent, 
and that the latter thesis is endorsed because sound is perceptible by means of the senses. 
However, it is possible to rearrange some of the fragments in their original order with 
reliance on two parameters, namely, the folio numbers and the numbers that mark the 
sections of the discussion. It is fortunate that Spitzer's transcription offal. 409 is preserved 
because it is the only fragment that allows us to coordinate folio numbers with numbers 
of text-sections. Cardinal to the following arrangement is the assumption that the present 
chapter could not have begun before fol. *397. If my allocation of the fragments preserved 
of the previous chapter entailing their placement after fol. 3 93 is accepted, this assumption 
is reasonable.

have been 17, 27, 3 7, etc. "17" can be excluded because it is preserved in frg. 136(5/2), and the sign for "30"

490 
The above assignment of the fragments is problematic for the following reasons. Frg. 
n25(1) belongs to fol. 405; therefore frg. 136(5/2) (containing section number 17) would 
have to belong to fol. *406. Frg. 19 (containing section number 22201 ) would therefore 
have to belong to fol. *407 and the remnant of fol. 40(7) be identified as part of fol. 40*8 
(together with frgs. n36 and 659); if the top left part of "7" or "8" was missing, it would 
have been impossible to distinguish between the two numbers. However, the resulting 
allocation not only goes against Spitzer's tentative reading of "40(7)," but would also 
leave very little room for section 28, starting on frg. 659 at the very end of fol. 40*8(?) 
according to my reconstruction of this leaf, and section 29, which is concluded at the very 
beginning of fol. 409. If my allocation of frg. 659 and with it the conclusion of section 27 
is accepted, less than a line would be left for sections 28 and 29. If my inclusion of frg. 
659 in this leaf is rejected owing to the uncertainty of the reading of "27 ,"about a line 
each would be available for sections 27, 28 and 29. 
If, on the other hand, one accepts Spitzer's reading of 40(7), together with my 
allocation of frg. 659 containing the section number 27, then frg. 19 (containing section 
number 22) would have to belong to fol. *406 and frg. 136(5/2), containing section 
number 17, to fol. 405. However, Spitzer read the number 405 on frg. n25(1) which does 
not seem to belong to the same leaf as frg. 136(5/2): frg. 660 seems to belong to the same 
leaf as frg. n25(1) (in both the topic of avivaditahetu is discussed), but cannot belong to 
the same leaf as frg. 136(5/2) because the line spacing of the two fragments diverges 
significantly; further, both frgs. 660 and n25(1) seem to have had only two lines of writing 
whereas frg. 136(5/2) has three lines. Therefore, it seems to me that the identification of 
40(7) as 408 is more probable. Thus, the following arrangement may be suggested:

I suggest calling this chapter "siidhana and upiilambha," not in an attempt to reconstruct 
its putative title, but simply to convey its subject matter. Similar discussions are known 
from the TS (ch. 2), the NS (section 5.1 onjiiti), the CS (Vi 8.36 on uttara), etc. In the 
following reconstruction I rely especially on the TS which displays the strongest similarity 
to the Spitzer fragments, because the fragments, albeit numerous, are very difficult to 
interpret on their own. 
The initial proof which recurs throughout the discussion is,: anityal:z sabdal:z, 
aindriyikatviid ghafiidivat. "Sound is impermanent because it is perceptible by means of 
the senses, just like a pot. "202 It is interesting to note that the common reason for 
impermanence in the later period, krtakatva, never occurs in the fragments, but this absence 
may well be accidental because so much of the discussion is missing. There is some 
indication that a reason such as krtakatva was employed by the author because fol. 398r3-
v 1 must have contained a definition of, or at least a statement about a cause ( ... ktira~Jnm 
u[kta]f!l/1/). Further, the word praya[tna] in fol. 403v1 is almost certainly part of a 
compound such as prayatnaja, prayatniiniintarfyaka or a similar expression. This points 
to the fact that causality was discussed in this chapter. The word dak~il)a in fol. 402v2 
probably refers to the right hom of a cow ( cf. also yathti gaur03 in fol. 403r2). The 
202 Cf. e.g., TS, p. 27.19-20: kiii ca sabdo 'nitya!J. kutaiJ. aindriyakatviit. ghafiidivad iti sthiipite .... Note that 
the Spitzer fragments consistently read aindriyika whereas in the dictionaries only aindriyaka is recorded. 
203 It is also possible to read gaur[a](va), but this reading is certainly less probable here. 

=== PAGE 150 ===
499 
argument is familiar from other sources and must have run as follows. The opponent 
interprets the word hetu as referring to a cause rather than to a reason (i.e., a material cause 
rather than an epistemological cause that reveals or makes something known) and argues 
that if this cause exists prior to what is to be proved (i.e., before its effect), it would not be 
a cause of anything and does not prove anything. If it exists after what is to be proved (i.e., 
after its effect), what is to be proved has already been established without it. If the two 
exist at the same time, they are not in a relation of cause and effect, just as the left and right 
horns of a cow do not cause one another. 204 In fol. 404r 1 the opponent seems to argue that 
in some cases one and the same entity is seen to be all-pervading, for instance, ether. From 
this it may follow that whatever is one and all-pervading is permanent (kvacic caikaf!l 
sarvvagataiJl dr~taiJl yathiikli[ s]( al:z)).205 
In fol. 404vl another familiar criticism is encountered: The denial of the similarity 
between sound and a pot as construed by the proponent ((ya)d uktaiJl ghatasiidharmy[ii]d 
anityal:z sabda [ i]ti tan niisti). The opponent may have mentioned certain properties that 
are not common to both sound and a pot, and claimed thereby that the example is not 
appropriate. For instance, the pot is corporeal, baked, perceptible by means of the sense of 
sight, etc., all properties not found in sound.206 In fol. 404v2 the opponent probably uses 
the same argument, but from the opposite angle. This time he affirms that there is a 
similarity between sound and a pot, and derives some inadmissible consequence from this 
similarity (( a)tha manyase asti ghafaslidharmyaf!l sabdasyeti prliptam an[y ] ... ). For 
instance, if the proponent has argued that sound is impermanent because it is perceptible 
by means of the senses I is produced I is produced by effort, just like a pot, the opponent 
could retort: If sound is impermanent because of its similarity to a pot, then sound is 
permanent because of its similarity to ether. The similarity consists, for instance, in the fact

prativiidf priiha. kif!Z hetuJ:t siidhyiit piirvaf!Z pasciid yugapad vii? yadi tiivad dhetuJ:t priik siidhyaii ca pasciit 
tadiisati siidhye, hetuJ:t kasya siidhakaJ:t? atha pasciit, siidhyaii ca priik, tadii siddhe siidhye kif!Z hetunii? atha

tasmiid yaugapadyaii cet tadii hetutviisambhavaJ:t. 
205 The defenders of the thesis that sound is permanent, notably the Mim~sakas, often argued that sound, 
being a quality of permanent ether, must itself be permanent. For instance TS, p. 23.20f.: nityaJ:t sabda 
iikiisasamiisritatviit. iikiiso nityaJ:t. yat kiiicid iikiisasamiisritaf!Z tan nityam. yathii paramiif}o/:t 
piirimiif}r}alyam. paramiif}ur nityaJ:t piirimiif}r}alyaii ca paramiif}usamiisritaf!Z, tasmiit piirimiif}rjalyaf!Z nityam. 
sabdo 'pi tadvad iikiisasamiisritatviin nityaJ:t. "Sound is permanent because it is supported by ether. 
Whatever is supported by ether is permanent. [Consider] for instance, the spherical form of an atom. The 
atom is permanent and the spherical form is supported by the atom. Therefore, the spherical form is 
permanent. Sound too, in a similar manner, is permanent because it is supported by ether."

nityaJ:t, ghafas tu prayatnenotpanno 'py anityaJ:t. tasmiic chabdo nityaJ:t. 

=== PAGE 151 ===
500 
that they are both incorporeal.207 The proponent's reply to these objections did not survive; 
he may have answered that the similarity is not based on any property whatsoever, but on 
the relevant property of being produced or being perceptible by means of the senses. 
In fol. 405rl the proponent seems to affirm that (similarity or a common property?) 
exists and asks rhetorically why (it) should not exist elsewhere ( ... astfti kasmiin na 
bhavaty anyatra). This is not so, the opponent presumably answers: What has been stated 
in this respect, namely, that there is a similarity between sound and a pot, is not (correct) 
(n[ii]tra yad uktarrt ghaJasiidharmya(rrt) sabdasyeti tan na[s](t)i). In fol. 405r2 the 
opponent may argue that owing to the alleged similarity to a pot, sound would also have 
to be seen.208 The argument could have run as follows: The pot is seen by means of the 
sense of sight, but sound is heard by means of the sense of hearing; thus, how could the two 
be similar just because they are perceptible? If they were similar, then sound too should be 
visible, and being visible it should be seen. 209 
In the next discemable argument (in fol. 405vl) the opponent seems to claim that 
if a reason is disputed (vivaditahetu), this is enough to invalidate a position. Unfortunately 
it is not clear in precisely which way the reason is disputed. One possible way to dispute 
it would be to argue that permanent things, such as universals, are also perceptible by 
means of the senses. Alternatively, if the reason were to be "because it is produced," one 
could dispute it, as the M'fma111sakas often did, by claiming that sound, being permanent, 
is merely revealed or manifested in various places at various times.210 That is, the 
M'fma111sakas may accept the general statement that whatever is produced is impermanent, 
but they deny that sound is produced. Another way to dispute the reason would be to argue 
that it is not the case that whatever is produced is destroyed because absence ( abhiiva) is 
also produced at a certain point in time and from that time onwards continues to exist 
forever. 211 
The Siddhantin questions the assertion that a thesis or a position is not established 
because it is disputed (kasmad vivaditahetupriiptatviid anupap( a)[ nn ] ... ). Further, he seems 
207 TS, p. 12.14-19: sabdo 'nityaJ:t prayatnotpannatvad yatha ghafaJ:t prayatnotpannaJ:t. utpannas ca vina~JaJ:t.

to deny that the reason is disputed, 212 or better, that if having an undisputed reason were the 
criterion for a correct thesis, sound would indeed be impermanent (( a)vivaditahetutve saty 
anitya eva sabda~ sytit). The purport of the argument is probably that a position should be 
considered valid not just because for some reason or other its reason is not disputed, but 
because its reason is valid. 
The next sentence (fol. 405v2) reads in Spitzer's transcription praptam 
aviva(
213ditahet)uprti[pt] Ill. In my opinion, hetu should probably be emended to hetu. To 
be sure, the reading hetu is not impossible. In spite of the neuter singular prtiptam, 
avivaditahetu may well be a masculine dual: praptam could govern a subsequent sentence 
whose subject or direct object is avivaditaheta. In this case, one could understand that as 
a result of the opponent's contention there would also be two undisputed reasons (one 
advanced by the proponent, the other by the opponent?). However, by analogy (and in 
opposition) to the previous line which reads vivaditahetuprtiptatvtid anupap( a)[ nn] ... , I 
prefer to read here avivaditahetuprtiptatvtid (*upapannam). It may be reminded that the 
distinction between short and long u is not always maintained in the manuscript. 
In fol. *406(?)r(?)l (= frg. 136a(5)1) we find the stereotypical expression atra 
vise~ahetur vticya~. The structure or purport of the phrase is clear, but unfortunately not 
its precise content. The proponent may be accusing the opponent of making an 
unreasonable exception for his thesis, i.e., all things are impermanent because of a certain 
property, but for some reason, claims the opponent, sound forms a special case so that (yat) 
it is permanent even though it possesses this property; the reason for such a partial 
distinction should be stated. I tentatively assume that the property in question is 
aindriyikatva. The second line would support this assumption and the remnants of the 
fragment do not point to another property. Cf. also the following argument in the TS, 
which, however, is not identical to the one in the Spitzer fragments: 
Furthermore, if [the following] is being established [by the proponent] "Sound is 
impermanent. Why? Because it is perceptible by means of the senses, like a pot etc.," the 
opponent retorts: Here, too, doubt is possible. If [something is] perceptible by means of 
the senses like a universal, then [it] would be permanent. If sound is perceptible by means 
of the senses, then, like a universal, it is permanent. If it were not permanent like a 
universal, then it would not be impermanent on account of the example of the pot.214

impermanence of sound .... " In the next line it is implied that the opponent's position leads

In text-section 18 (fol. *406(?)v(?) 1) the opponent claims that there is no example

216 Although the subject matter is evident (i.e., again the

On the next leaf (fol. 40*8(?) consisting offrgs. n25(2), n36 and 659) the opponent 
argues in the first line of the recto that even if the impermanence of sound were established 
by its similarity with a pot, (nevertheless its dissimilarity with permanent things) is not 
established because ... , and (thus) the impermanence of sound is not established. What has 
been said, namely, impermanence of(sound) because of the similarity to a pot ... (yady api 
ghafaslidharmylit siddhaf!l cchabdiinityatvam Ill dhyatvlid217 asiddham asiddha(f!l) ca 
sabda[ni](t)[y](a)[tv](af!l) yad ucyate ghafas[li]dh[a](r)my(li)[d] (a)n(i)ty ... ).On the 
same line a new argument begins ( = text-section 24/25 [?]), encapsulated in the word 
gurtavaidharmylit*. If I reconstruct this argument correctly, it refers to different types of 
qualities, especially in view of their mutual contradiction and possible destruction. The 
speaker seems to distinguish between virodhagu~J-a and bhlivagu~J-a and claim that (only) 
these two types of gurtas may be impermanent (iha218 virodhagurtabhlivagu~J-linlim 
anityatvli219 bhavati). One kind of destruction occurs because of a quality contradictory to 
another quality, for instance, the destruction of warm temperature by cold temperature. The 
second type of destruction of gurtas seems to be due to the destruction of their support 
( lisraya). Thus, when a pot is destroyed, its qualities, such as colour and form, are 
destroyed with it( ... gurtavinliso bhavati yathli ghafavinli(so) ... ).In the case of sound, 
however, neither of these two kinds of destruction can take place. There is no cause (such 
as anti-sound) that stands in contradiction to it, and its support cannot be destroyed because 
ether is permanent (na ca sab[d]as[y]a viro[dh](a)[k]lirartam asti kificin nlisraya?n?i ... ).

The word pravrttiprasaliga (fol. 40*8(?)vl) contains text-section 27 in a nutshell. 
It is difficult to determine in what sense the word prav.rtti is used here. Perhaps it refers to 
a continued activity. The proponent argues that if sound were permanent it would have to 
continue, i.e., continue to exist, as long as the activity which is its cause continues to exist 
because things to be realised by activity continue to exist as long as this activity is present, 
just as illumination continues to exist as long as a light is present (pravrttiprasa(ngli)t I 
yadi kvacid aindriyikam . ... y Ill [2] (pravr? )ttisadbhiiviit pravrttisiiddhyiiniim lirtthliniil!l 
sadbhiiv[ o] Ill pradfpasadbhiive prakiisasadbhiival:z ). Perhaps the proponent claims that the 
presence of a light too should be considered as a continued activity, and when this activity 
as cause is present, there is continued activity in the form of the presence of illumination. 
Thus, when there is presence of ether as the cause of sound, this presence should also be 
considered as a continued activity effecting the continuation of sound. However, this would 
result in the presence of sound even long after the drum, etc., has been played. This goes 
against our experience because the flow of sound today is not apprehended (tomorrow) 
(slipi pravrtti/:l pravrttau sety am[i? 1 . .Ill [dbh]livaJ:t prasaktas [t]./11 [3] ... yam adya 
sabdapravrttir iyal!l na gr[h]yat[ e) [te? 1 Ill). 
The third line contains the expression madhyagraha1Ja, which is recorded by MW 
and Apte in the sense of "the middle of an eclipse." I cannot accommodate this meaning 
into the present context. The expression could refer to perception of sound in the middle 
between its place of production and place of extinction, or to perception of the middle part 
of a word. 
Practically nothing remains of text-sections 28 and 29. In section 30, which may 
have continued up to fol. 409v 1, the topic of similarity is taken up again. The first word of 
this section, which was probably its keyword, reads in Spitzer's transcription 
[s](ii)dha(r)my(a)bh[ii]v[ii], 
but because the reading 
(a) 
is 
uncertain both 
siidharmyabhiivii( t) and siidharmyiibhiivii( t) are possible. In view of the next two lines 
these two possible readings may be understood as implying either that sound is similar to 
non-perceptible things or that it is not similar to perceptible things. Subsequently (fol. 
409r2) the opponent may have asked rhetorically: There is no (similarity between sound 
and perceptible things). How (could sound) be perceptible by means of the senses? How 
... ( n <ii> sti kuta/:l p [ u ]na[ r] a in[ dr] iyikatval!l kasmii II I). In 409v 1 the proponent probably 
summarizes the opponent's position or states its implication: You think that there is a 
similarity (of sound) with what is not being perceived ((a)nupalabhyamli[n](al!l?) 
[s](ii)[dh](ar)[m]y(a)[mf20 (a)st[f]ti manyase Ill). 
Fol. 409v2 may belong to text-section 31. There is not much to go by except the 
word vikalpa which, if one may rely on a passage in the TS which also seems to use this 
word, may suggest an option of two opposing alternatives derived from the same reason. 
vikalpakhal}t}ana is explained in the TS as the counter-establishing dissimilarity in the case 
220 Read anupalabhyamiinasiidharmyam or perhaps anupalabhyamiinena siidharmyam. 

=== PAGE 155 ===
504 
of similarity.221 If the proponent establishes that sound is impermanent because it is 
produced by effort like a pot, the opponent retorts that the two are also dissimilar and thus 
sound is permanent because it is produced by effort, whereas the pot is impermanent 
although it is produced by effort.222 This argument could easily be applied to the reason 
aindriyikatva which is used here. If this section continues to the next leaf ( 41 Or2), the word 
vi~ama could indicate the proponent's possible response, namely, that according to the 
opponent all things which are perceived by means of the senses are impermanent except 
sound which is supposed to be permanent, and that thus a reason for this unevenness should 
be stated. 
Pol. 410vl must belong to text-section 32 or 33. Here it is denied that the 
perception of the Self is a sensory perception (hy223 atm( a)pratyak~a[m](in)[ dr ](iya)(? )Ill). 
The relevance of this statement to the subject matter is obvious. If the atman, which is 
eternal, is perceived by means of the senses, the reason aindriyikatva cannot establish the 
impermanence of sound. 
Text-section 34 was perhaps summarized by means of the word anyabhavat, but 
this is not specific enough to gain further insight into the opponent's argument in this 
section. About fol. 411 nothing meaningful can be said except that the discussion on the 
similarity and dissimilarity between sound and a pot continues. The details, however, 
cannot be reconstructed. A small fragment (frg. 770) that bears the section number 38 and 
probably belongs to ca. fol. *412 may contain the remnants of a summarizing keyword on 
the other side, namely, 11/[ka]sabhavat*. This may be conjectured as vikasabhavat*. In 
view of the subsequent fragments 25, 41,53 and 142, which I assign to fol. *413, it seems 
quite possible that the identity of the opponent has already changed in frg. 770: while the 
opponent until now seems to have been a Mimatpsaka, the opponent in these fragments, 
where transformation (vikara) is prominently mentioned, seems to be a Sarp.khya. This fits 
well with the conjectured vikasa which, together with sankoca, is often used as an example 
for transformation. 
In frg. 25a3 a product of transformation is stated to be impermanent, not 
permanent.224 In 25b2, text-section 44 probably starts with the keyword prakrttiviktirli(t). 
The Sarp.khya may argue that sound is only a material transformation of prakrti which is 
eternal; assuming that there is no ontological difference between the basic matter and the 
various forms it takes, one can assert that sound too is permanent. At this point the 
Sarp.khya theory of satkaryavada, even if the term had not yet been, was probably 
considered. The Sarp.khya would argue that sound already exists in and is not really 
different from the eternal primordial matter ( cf. tasman na vyatirikto in frg. 53b, cf. p. 
221 TS, p. 14.24-25: siidharmye vaidharmyapratyavasthiinaTJl vikalpakha1,ujanam ucyate. 
222 TS, p. 15.3-7 quoted inn. 206 above. 
223 I assume that the sentence started with na hy. Otherwise the statement would be a purvapak~a statement. 
224 This statement must apply to sound (sabda), but "sabda" being a masculine noun the statement may be 
general: Whatever is a product of transformation is impermanent.

505 
496). A discussion of what it means to be destroyed in the Sarpkhya sense may have 
followed. 
It is difficult to determine how much longer the discussion with the Satpkhya 
extended. What remains of fol. 414 is too little to allow determination of a specific 
opponent or a specific argument. Unfortunately, the remaining fragments of the manuscript 
do not allow for further reconstruction of the dialectical portion. 
Finally, it may be mentioned that I could not assign a number of fragments relating 
to the topic of formal proof in debate to their original place in the manuscript. They 
certainly belong to the chapter on sadhana and upalambha, but they bear no clues as to 
folio number or text -section number. Therefore, they are neither discussed here nor 
reproduced below; they are frgs. 12(4), 24, 117, 150, 153,216, 307,431, 442(1), 533,597, 
605, 624, 663, 667, 676(?) (perhaps belonging to the chapter on sophistical debate partially 
reconstructed above) and 759.
